	 1.	 Distance and Resolution

	
1.	 The manner in which the eye makes units was fundamental to Gestalt 

psychology, and becomes essential to both the science of vision and visual 
technologies, which take advantage of these aspects to produce visual 

271
Notes to Chapter 1

representations. Thus it is no surprise that these aspects of unification of 
seemingly disparate parts becomes essential to a science of vision, e.g., in 
the early computational theory of vision in David Marr’s Vision. Indeed, 
Marr’s “underlying physical assumptions” include a specification of scale 
by the visual system (45).
	 2.	 While scientists separate out many more factors in delineating the object 

of study and what is being viewed, the definition provided here fits gener­
ally with the way the term “resolution” is used. For example, one definition 
used by geographers is “the smallest distinguishable parts in an object or 
sequence” (Lam and Quattrochi, “On the Issues of Scale,” 89). A similar 
definition has been used in metrology: “the smallest difference δ between 
indications that can meaningfully be distinguished” (Lira, “Resolution Re­
visited,” L14). In microscopy, the concept of resolution is defined likewise 
as “the ability to differentiate between two small, closely spaced objects” 
(Ryan et al., “Introduction,” 2).
	 3.	 This remixes Gregory Bateson’s definition of information as “a difference 

that makes a difference.” The point becomes one of the foundations for the 
scalar reconfiguration of objects in chapter 7.
	 4.	 For reasons that will become clear, it is essential that we reserve the term 

“Whole” here only for this largest-­scale perspective This use of “Whole” 
runs us up against a history of uses of “whole” in science that differs in 
essential ways from this definition (see Harrington, Reenchanted Science). 
Throughout this work I will mark this specialized notion of the Whole, 
All, and One with the capital in order to distinguish them from the more 
limited ways these words can be used.
	 5.	 See Bhagavad Gita, 12:3–­4. Definition from Monier Williams.
	 6.	 I am using “negation” primarily in the sense implied by traditions of 

negative theology, particularly Pseudo-­Dionysius: the act of voiding the 
essentiality granted to a category. Although the term is far more loaded, I 
suspect that this use fits to some degree with Hegel’s use of the term, since 
we are, in some way, coming to a “negation of the negation.”
	
7.	 Some of the theories listed here more or less consider this singularity as ten­

tative or functional operations. However, the problem, which we will face 
extensively in chapter 7, is that we are perpetually forgetting this secondary 
character of individuation and treating things as having an “in themselves.”
	 8.	 This idea has also been noted by Woods in “Epistemic Things,” 78–­79.
	 9.	 See especially Haldane, “On Being the Right Size”; Bonner, Why Size Mat­

ters; West, Scale.

	 3.	 Scope and Accumulation

	
1.	 If we already assume scale, the first (relations moving across a boundary) 

can be produced by going to a larger scale and the second (more already 
in a relation than is originally discerned) can be produced by going to a 
smaller scale. As an example of these together, one article tying together 
geomorphology and ecosystem ecology notes that “because most ecosys­
tem processes are scale dependent, the choice of boundaries is of profound 
importance to the conceptualization of an ecosystem and to the scope 
and validity of questions being asked within that ecosystem. Indeed, any 
conceptualization of an ecosystem requires constraining the spatial and 
temporal scales of analysis” (Post et al., “The Problem of Boundaries,” 111). 

273
Notes to Chapter 4

The turn to scale here arises from the need to delimit and account for the 
contingency and porousness of the attempt to define an ecosystem even 
according to geomorphology.
	 2.	 Haldane, “On Being the Right Size.”
	 3.	 From the perspective of Gaia theory, the Earth is already integrated into 

organism-­like feedback loops (see chapter 9). However, Vernadsky and 
Teilhard de Chardin push further, considering man part of an emerging 
noösphere, which suggests more integration is forthcoming. See Ver­
nadsky, “The Biosphere and the Noösphere”; Teilhard de Chardin, The 
Phenomenon of Man; Doyle, Darwin’s Pharmacy, 175.
	 4.	 Set theory usually bans the set of all sets because it creates a logical par­

adox. As we will discuss in chapter 7, we can say that there are two kinds 
of abstraction that must be distinguished if we are going to talk about the 
“highest order of abstraction”: abstraction in signs and abstraction about 
signs. Cantor’s “set of all sets” is an abstraction in signs. The highest order 
of abstraction meant here is not merely a sign (“set of all sets”) but a sign 
about signs: it is a glimpse of the process of abstracting itself whereby one 
designates sets. It is a sign that indicates something about set-­making, 
specifically the openness to anything that might be found in distinguishing 
sets. Thus, “set of all sets” as a term is of a different logical type than any 
set. We only think that this continues to produce paradox if we ascribe 
too much concreteness to the phrase “set of all sets” (Whitehead calls this 
problem “misplaced concreteness”).
	 5.	 Hilbert’s Hotel was formulated in 1924 but was popularized by Gamow in 

One, Two, Three . . . Infinity, 17–­18.

	 4.	 To the Bottom

	
1.	 The Planck length is mathematically derived from three known constants 

(the speed of light, the gravitational constant, and Planck’s constant). It 
is theoretically the smallest scale at which gravity would have an effect or 
where the known aspects of the universe would be relevant. This length is 
incredibly small: 1.616255 × 10⁻35 meters.
	 2.	 There are many hints of this formulation in physics, for example in Bohm 

and Hiley’s ontological interpretation of quantum physics. Chapter 14 
of The Undivided Universe hints at scale as relevant for the resolution of 
some paradoxes of quantum theory. For example, this thought experiment 
provides a way of conceptualizing the “jumps” that makes Bohm’s onto­
logical interpretation possible as opposed to Bohr’s epistemological one, 
allowing one to speak of a determinable state of a quantum even if it is not 

274
Notes to Chapter 4

predictable or controllable (see Bohm and Hiley, The Undivided Universe, 
25; see also chapter 7 here).
	 3.	 The cellular automata of Wolfram’s A New Kind of Science and the chemical 

creation myth of Kauffman (see At Home in the Universe) provide two 
other ways to think of this aggregation as a kind of patterning made possi­
ble by simple rules of interaction on a scale below (see chapter 9).
	 4.	 This point suggests a scalar aspect of the self-­assembly of components, e.g., 

in Foerster: “The form of the elementary building blocks influences the 
possible forms of self-­organization” (Beginning, 126). Similarly, Wolfram 
presents the possibility that “particles are analogs of the localized struc­
ture” within the kind of patterns he identifies in A New Kind of Science: “and 
if this is so, then it means that at the lowest level, the rules for the universe 
need make no reference to particular particles” (525).
	 5.	 For example, Bertalanffy: “Here, Newtonian mechanics and classical 

physics, as based upon these visualizable categories, are perfectly satisfac­
tory. They break down, however, if we enter universes to which the human 
organism is not adapted. This is the case, on the one hand, in atomic 
dimensions, and in the cosmic dimensions on the other” (General Systems 
Theory, 242). Bohm and Hiley make a similar point in their discussion 
of the difference between classical and quantum world (The Undivided Uni­
verse, 347).
	 6.	 Accounts of the genesis of quantum physics often note that statistical 

methods were essential to making the transition to the quantum (e.g., 
Stone, Einstein and the Quantum, 40–­41). Prigogine and Stenger’s Order out 
of Chaos begins with observations about probability to this effect: “Obvi­
ously, in addition to deterministic processes, there must be an element of 
probability involved in some basic processes” (xxvii).

	 6.	 In the Scalar Simulation

	
1.	 The term “zoom” is derived from the sound made by a fast-­moving object. 

In one sense, then, it is an appropriate metaphor, since the orders of mag­
nitude created by scale mean that we are changing perspective with greater 
and greater speed (of the sort performed in the first experiential origin). 
But, in another sense, it is inappropriate, since this is not a movement at 
all, but a change of resolution.

clear, but open up a few corpses and it starts to become clear” (146). But 
if going beneath the surface is not just a going into the body but smaller in 
scale, as it becomes in the subsequent decades following Bichat (especially 
in the development of cell theory and later with Pasteur), then we find an 
even more significant shift in medicine. We will discuss this in terms of cell 
theory in chapter 9.

	 11.	 Heraclitus, Fragment 50 (118 in the Wheelwright translation). Logos is 

an important term in the history of Greek philosophy and a particularly 
important term for Heraclitus. It is variously translated as “to speak,” 
“reason,” or “account.” For Heraclitus, Logos is the name for this larger 
structure of existence. I prefer this translation from the classicist Wheel­
wright; the point is much the same in Robinson’s more literal translation, 
but he parenthetically weakens the statement of oneness: “Not after 
listening to me, but after listening to the account [logos], one does wisely 
in agreeing that all things are (in fact?) one (thing)” (37, brackets in the 
original).

effect on the path of the particles even as those particles have particle-­

possibility that some lower-­scale wave behavior manifests as part of these 
larger-­scale particle attributes in the manner already described in 4.4–­12.

foundational to Deleuze and Derrida and becoming the basis for philos­

have focused here on Plato’s formulation of difference, because it provides 
a surprisingly relevant account of the role of difference that is appropri­
ate for this scalar account. While I’ll leave it to others to trace out how 
this account of difference may differ from these philosophers’, I can note 
that many emphases on difference, including Deleuze’s, tend to invoke 
difference as generative of being. In one sense, we can agree that difference 
is generative of being in the sense of the Many, but this generation does not 
preclude the openness toward that being already implied in Deleuze and 
Guattari’s plane of immanence. I’m arguing here that this openness is the 
predifferentiated One-­Whole. This is indeed why Deleuze and Guattari 
have been accused by many of being mystics (e.g., by Hallward—­see end­
note 96 for chapter 9 below).

	 63.	 I first encountered this statement that consciousness is always “conscious­

ness of ” in Merleau-­Ponty (Phenomenology of Perception, 43). It is echoed, 
albeit flipped onto a subject, in Morton’s Hyperobjects: “What is called 
consciousness is an aesthetic effect: it is consciousness-­for” (84). This 
distinction becomes fundamental in Gupta’s account of cit and conscious­
ness in Vedanta; she argues that this question of consciousness as strictly 
possessive is due to an assumption that consciousness must be intentional 
(Cit: Consciousness, 7, 101).
	64.	 This invocation of consciousness is always controversial, but I invoke 

it here for the necessity of accounting for the potential for any part of 
“what is” to be described in terms of response-­ability. Such invocations 
of “consciousness” as a general term are attempts to extend the scope 
of something we readily observe within observation—­that being 
aware is an attribute of reality. It is interesting how readily we accept a 
generalization of other ways of positing reality, such as energy, resistance, 
weight, tangibility (i.e., matter), or even agency, but resist so strongly 
the same kind of description for the aspect of reality most immediately 
apparent within the inquirer. Regardless, a more nuanced version of this 
notion of consciousness cannot begin if we just simply dismiss it offhand. 
To that end, Gupta’s Cit provides an essential exposition of this generalized 
notion of consciousness from an Advaitan perspective. See especially 
chapter 5.

we need to acknowledge that these relations are, in some sense, internal to

Rhetoric as examples. Many other studies by those trained in rhetoric have

	 60.	 Versluis, Platonic Mysticism, traces the origin of the term mysticism to 

Platonic traditions; he is less clear on whether the term ought to be applied 
directly to Plato, but others—­in a more critical vein—­have done so with 
ease (see, e.g., Russell, “Mysticism and Logic”), and we can accept, at the 
least, that Plato was read this way through the Neoplatonists, Pseudo-­
Dionysius, and into the Christian traditions as it is expounded later by 
Evelyn Underhill and others. On the origin of the term rhetoric, Schiappa 
has, with some controversy, claimed that Plato coined the term in the 
Gorgias, deriving it from the previous term rhetor as the speaker in the 
Assembly (see Schiappa, “Did Plato Coin Rhētorikē?”). Regardless of the 
validity of this claim, Plato’s notion of rhetoric, particularly in the Gorgias 
and the Phaedrus, has influenced all discussions of the term after him.

	 8.	 Most theories of scale used for cultural criticism are derived from geog­

raphers (see chapter 9, endnote 24). Another approach has been through 
Wallerstein’s world-­systems theory; this was central to the special issue of 
Modernism/Modernity, e.g., as engaged by Vadde’s “Scalability” (note that 
the geography conversations on scale have been infused with Waller­
stein since the 1980s [see Herod, Scale, 8]). Some work has usefully cited 
ecologists (e.g., T. Clark, “Scale”; Woods, “Scale Critique,” 136), but as 
M. T. Clarke and Wittenberg note, much of this work remains focused on 
the large scale (introduction, 24). Thus, the theories of scale used in these 
works remain limited, with two notable exceptions: Horton’s “Composing 
a Cosmic View” situates scale in relation to Paracelsus and Whitman, 
offering a broader view of scalar productions; and Woods’s “Epistemic 
Things” considers Rheinberger’s epistemic things in relation to objects 
from other scale domains.
	 9.	 I use “critical” here more in the negative, pejorative sense rather than the 

more formal sense developed in the last chapter.

Horton, “Composing a Cosmic View,” invokes Cosgrove.

By, 5.

	 37.	 In contrast, other depictions have attempted to confuse this view of scale, 

to different ends. Latour’s “Anti-­zoom” was written to accompany a 2014 
exhibit by Olafur Eliasson at the Fondation Louis Vuitton in Paris. Latour 
here wants to speak of connectivity but not scale, since he associates scale 
with hierarchy and power. Thus he describes Eliasson’s aim as a disori­
entation (“Anti-­zoom,” 101). In response, we can note that our account 
of scale shows that disorientation is not antithetical to scale but essential 
for its reorientation. As another contrast, the scalar exhibit by Stephen S. 
Young and Paul Kelly, Macro or Micro?, deliberately mixes up scales, plac­
ing together large-­scale and small-­scale imagery that looks similar in order 
to blur the differences in scale (see Young and Kelly, “Macro or Micro?,” 
253). Such blurring can be useful for identifying isomorphisms across 
scales and for showing how strange scalar views can be, but it will not 
teach us about the apparatus of scale itself that helps us account for these 
different perspectives.

	 10.	 Massumi, Parables for the Virtual, 195.
	 11.	 Bennett and Hacker call this ascription of attributes to the brain the 

“merelogical fallacy” of neuroscience, since it attempts to ascribe attributes 
derived from the whole human body to a part of that body (Philosophi­
cal Foundations of Neuroscience, 68–­74). But their articulation is largely 
situated in this-­scale terms: selecting one object on this scale (the brain) 
to stand in for another (the human organism). The basic point holds, 
perhaps, but thinking of the shift as body-­brain to neuron-­experience 
would alter their account, since this shift becomes two ways of describing 
the same thing rather than synecdochically selecting one part (brain) to 
fill in for something (body). Dennett’s response to Bennett and Hacker 
(reprinted in Neuroscience and Philosophy) provides a useful overview of 
his attempt to find other levels (what he calls the “sub-­personal” [“Philos­
ophy as Naive Anthropology,” 77–­79]) for describing attributes of mind. 
From my limited view, I only want to offer the question: how are these 

302
Notes to Chapter 12

levels made to relate to each other if we understand them as two ways of 
describing the same thing, at one point as an organism with a cognitive 
operation and at another point as neurons processing this cognition?
